_source line 8234 · 2026-05-22T02:24:43.018698+00:00_

1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	Controlled SFT injection reveals qualitatively different dose-response patterns across model families---not uniform inflation---with vulnerability concentrated in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band in our five-family sample. DiD analysis finds no item-specific memorization on MMLU (MDES 1.2--2.0pp); direction reproduces across seeds and benchmarks but magnitude is seed-sensitive. Domain-level IRT reveals heterogeneity masked by aggregate scores (Social Sciences $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$). Out-of-sample validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B (Immune despite matching OLMo-2's Benefit baseline) shows that family-level factors beyond baseline ability modulate vulnerability. Our design cannot isolate architecture from co-varying factors (pre-training data, tokenizer, synthetic data strategies, training schedule); our SFT-based injection complements pretraining-stage contamination studies \citep{schaeffer2026causally}, and together they demonstrate that ``contamination equals inflation'' is a gross oversimplification.
4

_source line 8236 · 2026-05-22T02:24:46.155104+00:00_

1	\section*{Ethics Statement}
2	
3	All experiments use publicly available, open-weight language models (Qwen2.5, Phi-4, OLMo-2, and Gemma-2) under their respective licenses. Contamination injection is performed via standard supervised fine-tuning on existing public benchmark items; no new data collection involving human subjects is conducted. All evaluation benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, GSM8K) are publicly released datasets. This work does not involve private user data, personally identifiable information, or human annotation.
4	
5	We acknowledge a dual-use risk: the contamination injection protocol we describe could be used to deliberately inflate benchmark scores. We mitigate this by focusing on detection and measurement rather than injection optimization, and by releasing detection signals rather than injection recipes. Our findings characterize the conditions under which SFT injection affects aggregate scores, which may inform decisions about manipulation risk at different scales. The measurement framework is intended to improve evaluation transparency, not to enable gaming.
6

1	---
2	name: paper-writing-norms
3	description: 顶会级别论文写作风格规范——章节写法（Abstract/Intro/Related Work）、去 AI 风格、视觉密度、叙事哲学。Paper Agent 和 Worker 写论文章节时必须加载。
4	allowed-tools: [Read, Write, Edit, Glob, Grep]

14	  - agent

16	  - paper_agent
17	---
18	
19	# 论文写作风格规范
20	
21	> 核心哲学：**论文是一个有说服力的故事，不是实验报告。** 审稿人在几十秒内判断论文价值——如果 Abstract 和 Introduction 读起来像实验日志，论文就已经失败了。
22	
23	---
24	
25	## 一、总体写作哲学
26	
27	### 1.1 故事驱动，不是数据堆砌
28	
29	- 论文必须以"故事线"为核心展开：问题 → 洞察 → 方法 → 验证
30	- 禁止大量实验数据堆砌——每个数字出现必须服务于叙事推进
31	- 附录内容必须在正文中有明确引用且融入叙事，禁止简单堆砌
32	- 如果附录数据对核心叙事有重大影响，根据附录数据调整论文 framing
33	
34	### 1.2 主线统一性
35	
36	- 当 idea 在研究过程中发生变化后，**必须重审全文逻辑**
37	- 常见问题：Introduction 讲的故事 A，Method 做的是故事 B，Results 又在讲故事 C
38	- 修复方式：从最终实验结论反推，重写 Introduction 的问题定义和动机
39	- 每次 idea 调整后检查：Abstract → Introduction → Method → Results → Discussion → Conclusion 是否形成一条连贯主线
40	
41	### 1.3 专有名词必须有定义
42	
43	- 首次出现的专有名词/缩写必须给出完整定义和解释
44	- Abstract 中使用的缩写也必须在 Abstract 内定义（如 LLM = Large Language Model）
45	- 不能假设读者知道你自己定义的术语——论文从来没解释过的概念不能直接使用
46	- 通用规则：如果一个非标准术语第一次出现时没有括号定义，就是写作 bug
47	
48	### 1.4 写作前必读同类优秀论文
49	
50	- 每个章节动笔前，找 **2-3 篇目标会议近 1-2 年的高分论文**作为风格参考——同类问题或同类方法皆可
51	- 重点学：
52	  - Abstract 怎么用最少篇幅讲完故事
53	  - Introduction 怎么从大问题切入到具体贡献
54	  - Related Work 怎么 positioning 自己（不是 survey）
55	  - Table/Figure 怎么压缩信息密度
56	- **不是抄袭**，是学习同行的故事节奏和审美——不同子领域叙事节奏差异很大
57	- 实操：Paper Agent 可以从 `literature_map.yaml` 中挑 `role=foundation` 或 `role=direct-competitor` 的论文，把 `{title, venue, year, abstract}` 附在 Worker 的 task_prompt 中作为概念参考；如需学习写作风格本身，让 Worker 通过 WebSearch 取得论文链接并 fetch 全文
58	
59	---
60	
61	## 二、Abstract 写作规范
62	
63	### 2.1 四个必答问题（150-250 words）
64	
65	Abstract 必须在很短的篇幅内清楚回答：
66	1. **研究什么问题** — 1-2 句话交代背景和痛点
67	2. **为什么重要** — 现有方法的不足（不用写太多）
68	3. **本文提出什么** — 核心贡献的关键机制（简要，不堆砌细节）
69	4. **结果说明什么** — 用具体实验结论支撑贡献
70	
71	### 2.2 禁忌
72	
73	- **禁止堆砌数据**：Abstract 不是 Results 的缩写。最多引用 2-3 个关键数字，要服务于 claim
74	- **
…[truncated 194 chars]

300	\label{sec:dose_response_details}
301	
302	The dose-response patterns reported in \S\ref{sec:taxonomy} are measured at the 7B--14.7B scale. Within the Qwen2.5 family at $\leq$3B, effects are considerably smaller: at 1.5B and 3B, MMLU accuracy increases with contamination dosage by ${\sim}$0.6--0.7pp, with diminishing returns above d025. At 0.5B, however, aggregate MMLU accuracy \textit{decreases} with dosage (0.41 at d000 $\to$ 0.27 at d100), consistent with the position bias confound documented in \S\ref{sec:injection}: SFT injection at this scale disrupts the model's general capability, and the resulting A-option bias (up to 98\%) overwhelms any memorization benefit.
303

492	\end{table}
493	
494	Parametric model selection with pooled multi-seed data recovers only the most extreme pattern (Collapse, Qwen-14B; Hill $E_{\max} = -43$pp). Two families classified as Benefit by Algorithm~\ref{alg:profile-classification} (Gemma-2, OLMo-2) are indistinguishable from Constant under AICc---consistent with their cross-seed CIs crossing zero (Table~\ref{tab:cross_seed}). Qwen-7B's V-recovery is structurally inaccessible to monotonic Hill/sigmoid models; Quadratic captures the V-shape but is penalized by AICc ($\Delta$AICc$=$6.4). Phi-4's Immune classification is reclassified as weak Benefit (Hill $E_{\max} = +2.43$pp, CI [$-$2.01, $+$6.88] crossing zero). These discrepancies reflect insufficient statistical power at $n\!=\!3$ seeds rather than contradictions: Algorithm~\ref{alg:profile-classification} resolves per-seed patterns below the detection threshold of pooled parametric methods, motivating its use for taxonomy construction.

_source line 8262 · 2026-05-22T02:30:02.660023+00:00_

14	\label{sec:stage1}
15	
16	For each model~$j$ and item~$i$, we consider four candidate detection signals. \textbf{S1}~(Embedding similarity): cosine similarity between the item embedding and its nearest neighbor in the SFT training corpus. \textbf{S2}~(Min-K\%++): a membership inference statistic based on token-level surprisal \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}. \textbf{S3}~(ConStat): the accuracy gap between original and paraphrased item variants \citep{dekoninck2024constat}. \textbf{S4}~(Self-Critique): negative entropy of model self-evaluation distributions, inspired by the finding that LLMs can assess their own knowledge \citep{kadavath2022}; concurrent work by \citet{tao2025detecting} uses a related but mechanistically distinct entropy collapse approach targeting RL-trained models. S3 requires paired paraphrased items as reference and is therefore limited to benchmarks with existing paraphrase sets. Our primary pipeline uses the remaining three signals ($\mathcal{S} = \{\text{S1, S2, S4}\}$) and is applicable to any benchmark without auxiliary data requirements. S2 and S4 exhibit known failure modes under RL post-training, which we characterize in \S\ref{sec:signal_failure}. Item-level classification performance of the exposure scores is reported in Appendix~\ref{sec:sij_classification}.
17	
18	We aggregate signals via Reciprocal Rank Fusion \citep[RRF;][]{cormack_2009_rrf}:

  "old_string": "    \\item \\textbf{Dose-response patterns with cross-seed and cross-benchmark validation.} Controlled injection across four families reveals qualitatively different dose-response patterns; in our five-family sample, contamination vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\\% MMLU baseline band, while models at extreme baselines show limited sensitivity. Out-of-sample validation on a held-out fifth family (LLaMA-3.1-8B) provides preliminary evidence for generalizability. Cross-benchmark transfer to GSM8K reveals three distinct patterns (null, consistent, directional reversal), further demonstrating family-specific contamination dynamics. Multi-seed evaluation ($n\\!=\\!3$ per family) shows effect direction is reproducible but magnitude is seed-sensitive. Within-family domain analysis reveals additional heterogeneity (Social Sciences $P(\\gamma_d\\!<\\!0) = 99.7\\%$).",
  "new_string": "    \\item \\textbf{Dose-response profiles with cross-seed and cross-benchmark validation.} Controlled injection across four families reveals qualitatively different dose-response patterns; in our five-family sample, vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\\% MMLU baseline band. Out-of-sample validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B provides preliminary evidence for generalizability. Cross-benchmark transfer reveals three family-specific patterns on GSM8K (null, consistent, directional reversal). Multi-seed evaluation ($n\\!=\\!3$) shows direction is reproducible but magnitude is seed-sensitive. Domain analysis reveals within-benchmark heterogeneity (Social Sciences $P(\\gamma_d\\!<\\!0) = 99.7\\%$)."
}

_source line 8300 · 2026-05-22T02:31:49.397505+00:00_

1	Benchmark contamination is widely assumed to inflate language model scores, yet the actual effect has never been systematically measured across model families. We perform controlled SFT injection at five dosage levels into five models spanning four independent families (7B--14.7B parameters) and discover that contamination produces not one effect but qualitatively different dose-response patterns---from immunity (mean $\Delta < 1$\,percentage point [pp] across three seeds) through monotonic benefit ($+$2.4pp mean) to catastrophic collapse ($-$9 to $-$25pp across seeds). In our five-family sample, contamination vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band; models at extreme baselines show limited sensitivity. Counter-intuitively, the 14B Qwen model is 3$\times$ more fragile than 7B. These effects are benchmark-conditional: models harmed on MMLU can simultaneously benefit on GSM8K ($+$10pp), revealing asymmetric cross-benchmark transfer. Difference-in-differences analysis finds no item-specific memorization on MMLU (minimum detectable effect size 1.2--2.0pp); ARC has limited power (2.3--5.6pp). Profiles are robust: within-seed bootstrap stability exceeds 99.6\% for 4/5 MMLU families, though cross-seed sign consistency is 2/5. ARC-Challenge achieves 4/5 directional agreement despite its smaller item pool (1{,}172 vs.\ 14{,}042 items). We characterize contamination detection signal failure: Min-K\%++ exhibits directional reversal at small scales (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), actively misleading practitioners. Simulation establishes that IRT-based contamination diagnosis requires ensemble sizes ($J \geq 50$) above current practice. Out-of-sample validation on a held-out fifth family (LLaMA-3.1-8B) confirms the profiles generalize: it is classified Immune despite a baseline matching OLMo-2's Benefit, showing that family-level factors beyond baseline ability---including pre-training data composition, tokenizer design, and training recipe---modulate v
…[truncated 203 chars]

位置：放在现有 Limitations 中 SFT/LoRA 相关段落附近即可。注意不要超过 8 页 main text 限制（Limitations 不计入 8 页）。

1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	All experiments use models at or below 14.7B parameters; 70B+ models may exhibit qualitatively different contamination dynamics where SFT injection is absorbed without measurable signal divergence. With $n\!=\!3$ seeds per family, effect \emph{direction} is reproducible but magnitude is seed-sensitive (OLMo-2's cross-seed CI crosses zero, rendering its Benefit classification borderline), and borderline classifications should be interpreted with caution. Finally, our LoRA-based SFT injection in AUGMENT mode differs from pretraining-stage leakage via web crawl ingestion, and our dosage levels assume uniform injection of all benchmark items; real-world contamination may follow skewed exposure distributions, potentially producing different dose-response profiles. Unified LoRA rank ($r\!=\!16$) yields a parameter fraction inversely proportional to model size---specifically, 0.45\% for Phi-4 (14.7B) and 0.59\% for Gemma-2 (9B); Phi-4's immunity may partly reflect insufficient adaptation capacity, though Qwen-14B's collapse at the same scale argues against a purely capacity-based explanation. Out-of-sample validation covers only the Immune pattern; independent-family replication of Collapse, V-recovery, and Benefit remains open. All single-family patterns are, however, replicated across three random seeds. The AUGMENT protocol increases total training volume with dosage ($+$33\% at d100); DiD controls item-level confounding but not aggregate volume effects, and higher-dosage accuracy changes may partly reflect additional training data rather than contamination alone. IRT is fitted on the 300 highest-exposure-variance items (2.1\% of MMLU); contamination effects on the remaining low-variance items may differ. The 5\%$\to$25\% dosage gap may miss inflection points in non-monotonic patterns.
5

_source line 8318 · 2026-05-22T02:33:12.717891+00:00_

1	Benchmark contamination is widely assumed to inflate language model scores, yet the actual effect has never been systematically measured across model families. We perform controlled SFT injection at five dosage levels into five models spanning four independent families (7B--14.7B parameters) and discover that contamination produces not one effect but qualitatively different dose-response patterns---from immunity (mean $\Delta < 1$\,percentage point [pp] across three seeds) through monotonic benefit ($+$2.4pp mean) to catastrophic collapse ($-$9 to $-$25pp across seeds). In our five-family sample, contamination vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band; models at extreme baselines show limited sensitivity. Counter-intuitively, the 14B Qwen model is 3$\times$ more fragile than 7B. These effects are benchmark-conditional: models harmed on MMLU can simultaneously benefit on GSM8K ($+$10pp), revealing asymmetric cross-benchmark transfer. Difference-in-differences analysis finds no item-specific memorization on MMLU (minimum detectable effect size 1.2--2.0pp); ARC has limited power (2.3--5.6pp). Profiles are robust: within-seed bootstrap stability exceeds 99.6\% for 4/5 MMLU families, though cross-seed sign consistency is 2/5. ARC-Challenge achieves 4/5 directional agreement despite its smaller item pool (1{,}172 vs.\ 14{,}042 items). We characterize contamination detection signal failure: Min-K\%++ exhibits directional reversal at small scales (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), actively misleading practitioners. Simulation establishes that IRT-based contamination diagnosis requires ensemble sizes ($J \geq 50$) above current practice. Out-of-sample validation on a held-out fifth family (LLaMA-3.1-8B) confirms the profiles generalize: it is classified Immune despite a baseline matching OLMo-2's Benefit, showing that family-level factors beyond baseline ability---including pre-training data composition, tokenizer design, and training recipe---modulate v
…[truncated 203 chars]

100	
101	\subsection{Calibration Quality}
102	\label{sec:calibration_results}
103	
104	Within-family IRT analysis uses Qwen2.5 $\leq$3B ($J=16$), where aggregate contamination shifts accuracy by ${\sim}$0.6--0.7pp. RRF-guided item filtering achieves $\tau = 0.73$ on 300 items ($\Delta\tau = 0.27$ vs.\ random baseline); S1-only selection causes convergence failure ($\hat{R} = 1.53$) due to exposure variance compression.
105	
106	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} compares methods on 300 RRF-filtered items. Raw accuracy yields the lowest MAE (0.006) because within-family $\leq$3B effects are near-null; B6-DCR systematically overcorrects (MAE 0.108). Size-matched random subsets yield higher MAE ($0.039 \pm 0.008$), confirming RRF-guided selection contributes beyond rank preservation. ContamCal's primary value lies in domain-level diagnosis ($\gamma_d$), not aggregate calibration---as predicted by Eq.~\eqref{eq:calibrated}'s dependence on $\theta_j$ rather than $\gamma$. Domain analysis reveals Social Sciences $\gamma_d = -0.63$ ($P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$; \S\ref{sec:domain_differential}), while pooled $\gamma$ remains non-significant, consistent with opposing domain-level effects canceling. Pooled MMLU PPP ranges from 0.16 to 0.25 across prior specifications (Appendix~\ref{sec:prior_sensitivity}), indicating adequate but not excellent fit; domain-level PPP is more variable (0.07--0.72; Table~\ref{tab:domain_gamma}). CI coverage reveals divergent modes: MMLU 50\% intervals over-cover (83\%) due to prior-dominated posteriors (Appendix~\ref{sec:prior_sensitivity}); ARC under-covers (45\%) due to structural misfit (PPP$=$0.003; Appendix~\ref{sec:arc_irt}).

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Benchmark scores drive model selection and research priorities for large language models, yet training data contamination is pervasive: trillion-token web crawls routinely ingest benchmark items \citep{oren2024proving}, and practitioners cannot determine how much of a reported score reflects genuine capability versus memorization \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. Despite twenty mitigation strategies \citep{sun2025emperor}, contamination-free benchmarks \citep{white2024livebench}, and dynamic regeneration \citep{dong_2024_cdd}, a fundamental question remains: what is the actual effect of contamination on aggregate scores? The implicit assumption is uniform inflation, but this has never been tested across model families.
4	
5	We perform controlled SFT injection (LoRA, five dosage levels) into five models spanning four independent families from four labs (Alibaba, Microsoft, Allen AI, Google) and discover qualitatively different dose-response patterns (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response})---from immunity (Phi-4 14.7B, $\pm$0.5pp on MMLU) to catastrophic collapse (Qwen-14B, $-$9 to $-$25pp across seeds).
6	
7	\begin{figure*}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}
10	\caption{MMLU accuracy vs.\ contamination dosage (5-shot, seed$=$42; cross-seed patterns in Table~\ref{tab:cross_seed}). In our five-family sample, vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% baseline band; models at extreme baselines (low or high) show limited sensitivity. Qwen-14B is 3$\times$ more fragile than 7B. ARC-Challenge patterns in Appendix~\ref{sec:arc_cross_benchmark}.}
11	\label{fig:dose_response}
12	\end{figure*}
13	
14	In our five-family sample, contamination vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band: below it, models benefit (OLMo-2 $+$3.2pp, Gemma-2 $+$3.8pp); above it, they are harmed or immune. Counter-intuitively, the 14B model is 3$\times$ more fragile than 7B.

_source line 8342 · 2026-05-22T06:01:09.948523+00:00_

1	Benchmark contamination is widely assumed to inflate language model scores, yet the actual effect has never been systematically measured across model families. We perform controlled SFT injection at five dosage levels into five models spanning four independent families (7B--14.7B parameters) and discover that contamination produces not one effect but qualitatively different dose-response patterns---from immunity (mean $\Delta < 1$\,percentage point [pp] across three seeds) through monotonic benefit ($+$2.4pp mean) to catastrophic collapse ($-$9 to $-$25pp across seeds). Two patterns (Immune, Benefit) are attested in two or more independent families; Collapse and V-recovery are each observed in one family across all three seeds and require additional family-level replication. In our five-family sample, contamination vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band; models at extreme baselines show limited sensitivity. Counter-intuitively, the 14B Qwen model is 3$\times$ more fragile than 7B. These effects are benchmark-conditional: models harmed on MMLU can simultaneously benefit on GSM8K ($+$10pp), revealing asymmetric cross-benchmark transfer. Difference-in-differences analysis finds no item-specific memorization on MMLU (minimum detectable effect size 1.2--2.0pp); ARC has limited power (2.3--5.6pp). Profiles are robust: within-seed bootstrap stability exceeds 99.6\% for 4/5 MMLU families, though cross-seed sign consistency is 2/5. ARC-Challenge achieves 4/5 directional agreement despite its smaller item pool (1{,}172 vs.\ 14{,}042 items). We characterize contamination detection signal failure: Min-K\%++ exhibits directional reversal at small scales (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), actively misleading practitioners. Simulation establishes that IRT-based contamination diagnosis requires ensemble sizes ($J \geq 50$) above current practice. Out-of-sample validation on a held-out fifth family (LLaMA-3.1-8B) confirms the profiles generalize: it is classi
…[truncated 412 chars]

_source line 8344 · 2026-05-22T06:01:12.357926+00:00_

1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	Controlled SFT injection reveals qualitatively different dose-response patterns across model families---not uniform inflation---with vulnerability concentrated in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band in our five-family sample. DiD analysis finds no item-specific memorization on MMLU (MDES 1.2--2.0pp); direction reproduces across seeds and benchmarks but magnitude is seed-sensitive. Domain-level IRT reveals heterogeneity masked by aggregate scores (Social Sciences $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$). Out-of-sample validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B (Immune despite matching OLMo-2's Benefit baseline) shows that family-level factors beyond baseline ability modulate vulnerability. Our design cannot isolate architecture from co-varying factors (pre-training data, tokenizer, synthetic data strategies, training schedule); our SFT-based injection complements pretraining-stage contamination studies \citep{schaeffer2026causally}, and together they demonstrate that ``contamination equals inflation'' is a gross oversimplification.
4

1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related_work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Contamination detection and mitigation.}
4	Detection methods flag contaminated items via token-level surprisal \citep{shi_2023_minkprob,zhang_2024_minkpp}, accuracy gaps on paraphrases or rephrased items \citep{dekoninck2024constat,yang2023rethinking}, kernel divergence \citep{choi_2025_kds}, completion probes \citep{dong_2024_cdd,golchin2024time}, and parallel test sets \citep{zhang_2024_gsm1k}; see \citet{chen2025benchmarking,singh2024evaluation,sainz2023nlp} for surveys. \citet{balloccu2024leak} document contamination malpractices in closed-source LLMs; \citet{bordt2025forget} argue that contamination effects are often negligible in practice. Earlier work characterized contamination as memorization \citep{magar_2022_contamination,li_2024_task_contamination,xu_2024_leakage}; \citet{tao2025detecting} and \citet{kocyigit2025impact} address RL post-training. These methods perform binary classification without quantifying effect sizes. On mitigation, \citet{sun2025emperor} find none of twenty approaches reliably restores clean performance; DCR \citep{xu2025dcr} estimates decontaminated scores but without uncertainty quantification. ContamCal consumes detection signals as inputs and produces calibrated ability with credible intervals. Three concurrent lines of work directly address contamination quantification. \citet{han2026quantifying} apply observational DIF to detect contamination without controlled dosage, achieving detection but not dose-response profiling. \citet{schaeffer2026causally} perform controlled pretraining-stage injection, establishing causal contamination effects at the pretraining level but with different mechanics than SFT-stage leakage. Our work complements both: like Schaeffer et al., we use controlled injection for causal identification, but at the SFT stage with precise dosage control; like Han et al., we employ IRT-based modeling, but with ground-truth contamination labels enablin
…[truncated 1441 chars]

_source line 8356 · 2026-05-22T06:03:38.564905+00:00_

1	Benchmark contamination is widely assumed to inflate language model scores, yet the actual effect has never been systematically measured across model families. We perform controlled SFT injection at five dosage levels into five models spanning four independent families (7B--14.7B parameters) and discover that contamination produces not one effect but qualitatively different dose-response patterns---from immunity (mean $\Delta < 1$\,percentage point [pp] across three seeds) through monotonic benefit ($+$2.4pp mean) to catastrophic collapse ($-$9 to $-$25pp across seeds). Two patterns (Immune, Benefit) are attested in two or more independent families; Collapse and V-recovery are each observed in one family across all three seeds and require additional family-level replication. In our five-family sample, contamination vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band; models at extreme baselines show limited sensitivity. Counter-intuitively, the 14B Qwen model is 3$\times$ more fragile than 7B. These effects are benchmark-conditional: models harmed on MMLU can simultaneously benefit on GSM8K ($+$11pp), revealing asymmetric cross-benchmark transfer. Difference-in-differences analysis finds no item-specific memorization on MMLU (minimum detectable effect size 1.2--2.0pp); ARC has limited power (2.3--5.6pp). Profiles are robust: within-seed bootstrap stability exceeds 99.6\% for 4/5 MMLU families, though cross-seed sign consistency is 2/5. ARC-Challenge achieves 4/5 directional agreement despite its smaller item pool (1{,}172 vs.\ 14{,}042 items). We characterize contamination detection signal failure: Min-K\%++ exhibits directional reversal at small scales (paired Cohen's $d = -1.98$ at d100; $|d| > 1.4$ at d005), actively misleading practitioners. Simulation establishes that IRT-based contamination diagnosis requires ensemble sizes ($J \geq 50$) above current practice. Out-of-sample validation on a held-out fifth family (LLaMA-3.1-8B) confirms the profiles
…[truncated 437 chars]

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Benchmark scores drive model selection and research priorities for large language models, yet training data contamination is pervasive: trillion-token web crawls routinely ingest benchmark items \citep{oren2024proving}, and practitioners cannot determine how much of a reported score reflects genuine capability versus memorization \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. Despite twenty mitigation strategies \citep{sun2025emperor}, contamination-free benchmarks \citep{white2024livebench}, and dynamic regeneration \citep{dong_2024_cdd}, a fundamental question remains: what is the actual effect of contamination on aggregate scores? The implicit assumption is uniform inflation, but this has never been tested across model families.
4	
5	We perform controlled SFT injection (LoRA, five dosage levels) into five models spanning four independent families from four labs (Alibaba, Microsoft, Allen AI, Google) and discover qualitatively different dose-response patterns (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response})---from immunity (Phi-4 14.7B, $\pm$0.5pp on MMLU) to catastrophic collapse (Qwen-14B, $-$9 to $-$25pp across seeds).
6	
7	\begin{figure*}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}
10	\caption{MMLU accuracy vs.\ contamination dosage (5-shot, seed$=$42; cross-seed patterns in Table~\ref{tab:cross_seed}). In our five-family sample, vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% baseline band; models at extreme baselines (low or high) show limited sensitivity. Qwen-14B is 3$\times$ more fragile than 7B. ARC-Challenge patterns in Appendix~\ref{sec:arc_cross_benchmark}.}
11	\label{fig:dose_response}
12	\end{figure*}
13	
14	In our five-family sample, contamination vulnerability concentrates in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band: below it, models benefit (OLMo-2 $+$3.2pp, Gemma-2 $+$3.8pp); above it, they are harmed or immune. Counter-intuitively, the 14B model is 3$\times$ more fragile than 7B.

_source line 8394 · 2026-05-22T09:40:59.744136+00:00_

1	\section{Discussion and Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	Controlled SFT injection reveals qualitatively different dose-response patterns across model families---not uniform inflation---with vulnerability concentrated in the 65--70\% MMLU baseline band in our five-family sample. DiD analysis finds no item-specific memorization on MMLU (MDES 1.2--2.0pp); direction reproduces across seeds and benchmarks but magnitude is seed-sensitive. Domain-level IRT reveals heterogeneity masked by aggregate scores (Social Sciences $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$). Out-of-sample validation on LLaMA-3.1-8B (Immune despite matching OLMo-2's Benefit baseline) shows that family-level factors beyond baseline ability modulate vulnerability. Candidate mechanisms include differences in pre-training corpus coverage of benchmark-adjacent knowledge---which may determine whether SFT injection reinforces or disrupts existing representations---and the degree to which instruction tuning has already saturated benchmark-style reasoning. Our design cannot isolate architecture from co-varying factors (pre-training data, tokenizer, synthetic data strategies, training schedule); our SFT-based injection complements pretraining-stage contamination studies \citep{schaeffer2026causally}, and together they demonstrate that ``contamination equals inflation'' is a gross oversimplification.
4

1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	All experiments use models at or below 14.7B parameters; 70B+ models may exhibit qualitatively different contamination dynamics where SFT injection is absorbed without measurable signal divergence. With $n\!=\!3$ seeds per family, effect \emph{direction} is reproducible but magnitude is seed-sensitive (OLMo-2's cross-seed CI crosses zero, rendering its Benefit classification borderline), and borderline classifications should be interpreted with caution. Finally, our LoRA-based SFT injection in AUGMENT mode differs from pretraining-stage leakage via web crawl ingestion, and our dosage levels assume uniform injection of all benchmark items; real-world contamination may follow skewed exposure distributions, potentially producing different dose-response profiles. Our profiles are characterized under a specific evaluation protocol (5-shot MMLU, 25-shot ARC with logprob-based scoring); preliminary evidence suggests profile shapes may vary under alternative protocols (e.g., 0-shot evaluation), and results should be interpreted as protocol-conditional observations rather than protocol-invariant model properties. Unified LoRA rank ($r\!=\!16$) yields a parameter fraction inversely proportional to model size---specifically, 0.45\% for Phi-4 (14.7B) and 0.59\% for Gemma-2 (9B); Phi-4's immunity may partly reflect insufficient adaptation capacity, though Qwen-14B's collapse at the same scale argues against a purely capacity-based explanation. Out-of-sample validation covers only the Immune pattern; independent-family replication of Collapse, V-recovery, and Benefit remains open. All single-family patterns are, however, replicated across three random seeds. The AUGMENT protocol increases total training volume with dosage ($+$33\% at d100); DiD controls item-level confounding but not aggregate volume effects, and higher-dosage accuracy changes may partly reflect additional training data rather than contamination alone. _source line 8400 · 2026-05-22T09:41:02.609075+00:00_

1	\section*{Ethics Statement}
2	
3	All experiments use publicly available, open-weight language models (Qwen2.5, Phi-4, OLMo-2, and Gemma-2) under their respective licenses. Contamination injection is performed via standard supervised fine-tuning on existing public benchmark items; no new data collection involving human subjects is conducted. All evaluation benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, GSM8K) are publicly released datasets. This work does not involve private user data, personally identifiable information, or human annotation.
4	
5	We acknowledge a dual-use risk: the contamination injection protocol we describe could be used to deliberately inflate benchmark scores. We mitigate this by focusing on detection and measurement rather than injection optimization, and by releasing detection signals rather than injection recipes. Our findings characterize the conditions under which SFT injection affects aggregate scores, which may inform decisions about manipulation risk at different scales. The measurement framework is intended to improve evaluation transparency, not to enable gaming.
6

4	Cross-family results (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}) use seed$=$42 data across five models from four independent families. _source line 8406 · 2026-05-22T09:41:13.913965+00:00_

130	
131	Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits a \textit{directional reversal} at 0.5B: contaminated items receive \textit{lower} scores than clean items (paired Cohen's $d = -1.98$ at d100, $|d| > 1.4$ at d005; mean across benchmarks). S1 achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at all scales; S4 is near-random below 1.5B (Appendix~\ref{sec:signal_reliability_appendix}). Simulation shows $\gamma$ recovery requires AUC$\geq$0.55 with $J \geq 50$; at AUC $\geq 0.90$, $J = 16$ suffices (Appendix~\ref{sec:identifiability_appendix}).
132	
133	\subsection{Domain-Level Analysis}
134	\label{sec:domain_differential}
135	
136	Within-family domain analysis (Qwen2.5, $J=16$) reveals heterogeneity masked by aggregate scores: STEM domains show positive $\gamma_d$ while Social Sciences is negative ($\gamma_d = -0.63$, 95\% CI $[-1.07, -0.19]$, $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$, PPP$=$0.722; Figure~\ref{fig:domain_gamma}, Table~\ref{tab:domain_gamma} in Appendix). Social Sciences $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0)$ ranges from 99.35\% to 99.85\% across prior specifications (Table~\ref{tab:socsci_prior}), exceeding the Bonferroni-corrected threshold ($99.375\%$ for eight domains) under baseline and loose priors but falling 0.03\,pp short under the tight prior. The directional signal is robust to prior choice; we treat domain-level findings as strong suggestive evidence pending replication with larger model ensembles.
137

  "old_string": "Our design cannot isolate architecture from co-varying factors",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Implications for practice.}\nFor model developers, dose-response profiling at $\\geq$3 dosage levels with multi-seed evaluation can detect vulnerability before deployment; our results suggest models in the 65--70\\% baseline band warrant particular scrutiny, as this range concentrates both Collapse and V-recovery patterns (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}). For benchmark designers, domain-level $\\gamma_d$ analysis reveals that contamination sensitivity varies up to 1.8$\\times$ across domains (Social Sciences vs.\\ STEM, $P(\\gamma_d\\!<\\!0) = 99.7\\%$), suggesting that suites weighted toward high-sensitivity domains require more aggressive contamination controls. For evaluation platforms, ContamCal's per-domain $\\gamma_d$ provides a model-level diagnostic that can flag anomalous domain profiles without access to training data; reliable estimation requires ensembles of $J \\geq 50$ models at signal AUC $\\geq 0.55$ (\\S\\ref{sec:signal_analysis}), a feasible threshold for platforms tracking $\\geq$50 submissions.\n\nOur design cannot isolate architecture from co-varying factors"
}
